# 2026-11-12

## TOPIC 1 — Lightroom Can Now Open Closed Eyes (Adobe MAX 2026)

### What Happened
At Adobe MAX 2026 in Miami Beach (Nov 10), Adobe rolled out a big batch of AI updates. For photographers the headline is Lightroom's new "Open Eyes" tool: it fixes closed eyes by using another photo of the same person from your library (you pick the reference or Lightroom finds one). It's in beta / early access, uses Generative Credits, and saves the fix as a new DNG. Lightroom also got "Glow" (Bloom, Diffusion, Halation, Motion, Streak), and Photoshop added Relight and Generative Restyle.

### Why It Matters
Closed eyes is the #1 group-shot killer at events, weddings and corporate shoots. Until now the fix was a manual head-swap in Photoshop. A one-click fix changes how much time event photographers spend in post — but it also raises the "is this still a real photo?" question, and it costs credits.

### Why It Suits Me
I shoot events for Pixel With Bliss — group photos and blinks are a real, everyday problem for me. I can speak to it as a working event photographer, not just someone reading the news.

### Hook
"Lightroom can now open your client's eyes — and I've got mixed feelings about it."

### Talking Points
- Open Eyes uses a different photo of the same person as a reference to fix blinks — it's in beta and uses Generative Credits.
- Lightroom also got Glow (halation/diffusion film-style effects), and Photoshop got Relight to move and add light after the shot.
- It saves time, but you still need to nail the shot — and be honest with clients about AI edits.

### My Angle
As an event photographer: this is a genuine time-saver for group shots, but it shouldn't replace good technique (shoot bursts, call "eyes open on three"). Raise the ethics question: is swapping eyes from another frame any different from the head-swaps we've done in Photoshop for years? Ask the audience where they draw the line.

### 30–60 Second Script
Lightroom can now open your client's eyes — and I've got mixed feelings about it.

So at Adobe MAX this week, Adobe announced a Lightroom feature called Open Eyes. If someone blinks in a photo, Lightroom grabs another shot of that same person from your library and uses it to fix the eyes. It's in beta right now, and it uses Generative Credits.

As someone who shoots events — this is huge. Group photo, twenty people, there's always that one person who blinks. Before, that meant a manual head swap in Photoshop.

They also added Glow — think film-style halation — and Photoshop got Relight, which lets you add and move light after the shot.

But here's my take: this doesn't replace good habits. I still shoot bursts and say "eyes open on three."

And honestly — is fixing a blink with the same person's real eyes cheating? Or is it just the new head swap? Let me know where you draw the line.

## TOPIC 2 — Claude Can Now Open Canva, Adobe & Notion Inside the Chat

### What Happened
On Nov 10, 2026, Anthropic launched "interactive connectors": supported apps can now open beside your Claude chat (or full screen) and Claude can make edits in them live — with your approval. Named launch apps include Canva, Adobe, Notion, Dropbox, Asana, Box, Atlassian, HubSpot and Webflow; Anthropic's example for Adobe is retouching/editing photos and editing PDFs. It's rolling out on web and desktop across all plans. Same day, Anthropic also launched Claude Sheets and Whiteboards (beta, paid plans) and previewed Claude Meetings.

### Why It Matters
Creators juggle 5+ tabs per post — script in one app, design in Canva, notes in Notion. Having the AI work inside those tools instead of just giving you text to copy-paste is a real workflow shift, and it's built on MCP, the same standard used by automation tools.

### Why It Suits Me
I'm a solo creator learning AI automation and I already use Claude, Canva and Notion-style tools to plan content. This is exactly the "AI workflow for small creators" lane I want to own.

### Hook
"Claude can now open Canva right inside the chat — and actually edit your design while you watch."

### Talking Points
- Apps like Canva, Adobe and Notion now open inside Claude, and Claude edits them live — you approve its actions.
- Rolling out on web and desktop for all plans, but you need the new Claude experience, so you might not see it yet.
- The big shift: AI stops being a chatbot you copy from and becomes something that works inside your tools.

### My Angle
From a solo-creator point of view: the win isn't "AI makes my designs" — it's cutting the copy-paste between apps. Be honest that it's rolling out and still early; frame it as "here's what I'm going to test for my content workflow" and invite people to follow along.

### 30–60 Second Script
Claude can now open Canva right inside the chat — and actually edit your design while you watch.

This week Anthropic launched what they call interactive connectors. Basically, apps like Canva, Adobe, Notion and Dropbox can now open right next to your Claude chat, and Claude can make changes in them live. You approve what it does.

Their Adobe example is literally retouching and editing photos.

Why do I care? Because as a solo creator, half my time is copy-pasting. Script here, caption there, design in Canva, notes somewhere else. If the AI can work inside those apps, that's hours back every week.

Now, quick reality check — it's rolling out gradually on web and desktop, so you might not have it yet.

I'm going to test it on my own content workflow and show you what actually works. Follow if you want to see that.

## TOPIC 3 — You Can Now Claim Your Business Inside ChatGPT

### What Happened
On Nov 10, 2026, OpenAI launched "Businesses in ChatGPT" in beta. Through a new Business Hub, verified businesses can claim their profile, update their details and product info, and see how often ChatGPT mentions them, website clicks, and topics they're cited for. Some U.S. e-commerce and online-service businesses can also build a "Business Agent" customers can chat with, and businesses can sponsor those agents as ads.

### Why It Matters
People are starting to ask ChatGPT for recommendations — "best event photographer in Toronto" — the way they used to ask Google. This is the start of "Google Business Profile, but for AI." Small businesses and creators who get in early can control what ChatGPT says about them.

### Why It Suits Me
I'm building Pixel With Bliss from zero and trying to land my first clients — how clients find small creative businesses is directly my problem. I can talk about it as a small business owner checking whether I can claim my own profile.

### Hook
"ChatGPT just became the new Google Business Profile — and most small businesses have no idea."

### Talking Points
- OpenAI's new Business Hub lets verified businesses claim their ChatGPT profile and update their info — it's in beta.
- You can see how often ChatGPT mentions you and how many clicks you get from it.
- Eligibility is still limited (some features U.S.-only), so check now — being early matters.

### My Angle
Small-business owner perspective: "Clients used to Google us — now they're asking AI." Don't overclaim ranking benefits (OpenAI hasn't said claiming boosts recommendations). Position it as: control your info, watch the data, and check eligibility — and do a follow-up showing whether a Toronto photography business like mine can claim yet.

ChatGPT just became the new Google Business Profile — and most small businesses have no idea.

Why does this matter? Because people are starting to ask ChatGPT stuff like, "Who's a good event photographer in Toronto?" — instead of Googling it.

Now, to be clear — it's in beta, some features are U.S.-only right now, and OpenAI hasn't said claiming your profile gets you recommended more.

But as someone building a photography business from scratch, I'm checking if I can claim mine today. If you run a small business, you should too.

Want me to show you how it goes? Comment "profile."

# 2026-11-13

## TOPIC 1 — YouTube Studio Can Now Turn Your Podcast Into Vertical Shorts

### What Happened
On Nov 12, 2026, YouTube posted an update (TeamYouTube, YouTube Help Community) saying the "video clips" tool inside YouTube Studio can now create Shorts, not just regular clips. You open a long video or podcast episode, pick a segment from the transcript or timeline, and YouTube reframes your 16:9 footage into a vertical Short with automatic captions. The manual tool is described as available globally; AI-suggested clip moments and outlines are limited to 10 countries (including Canada and the US) and 7 languages.

### Why It Matters
Clipping long videos into Shorts is something creators usually pay for (Opus Clip-style tools) or spend hours doing in an editor. Now a basic version is built right into YouTube Studio, for free, and the Shorts link back to the full video. It also changes what podcast/long-form clients will expect when they hire an editor.

### Why It Suits Me
I edit in DaVinci Resolve and create short-form content myself, and I'm trying to land clients in Toronto. Podcasters and businesses with long-form video are exactly the kind of people who might hire me — so I can explain what the free tool does, where it falls short, and where a human editor still adds value. Canada is one of the countries that gets the AI suggestions.

### Hook
"YouTube just added a free button that turns your podcast into Shorts — so do you still need an editor?"

### Talking Points
- YouTube Studio's clips tool can now make vertical Shorts from your long videos and podcasts, with auto-captions — no outside app.
- In Canada and the US you also get AI-suggested moments; everywhere else you pick the segment yourself.
- It's an auto-crop with a blurred background and basic captions — great for volume, but it won't do custom framing, B-roll, sound design or a strong edit.

### My Angle
As someone who edits for a living (well, building toward it): this is good news, not a threat. Use the free tool for quantity and testing which moments land, then pay a human (or spend your own time) polishing the top 1–2 clips that are actually performing. Free tools raise the floor; they don't replace taste. And for small businesses in Toronto with a podcast, this removes the excuse for not posting Shorts at all.

### 30–60 Second Script
YouTube just added a free button that turns your podcast into Shorts. So — do you still need an editor?

Here's what changed. Inside YouTube Studio there's a clips tool. As of this week, it can make Shorts. You open your long video, pick a section from the transcript, and YouTube flips it to vertical and adds captions for you. If you're in Canada or the US, it'll even suggest the moments it thinks are best.

Now, as an editor, here's my honest take. This is a great starting point. If you have a podcast and you're posting zero Shorts, you've got no excuse anymore. Make ten of them, see what people actually watch.

But it's an auto-crop. Blurred background, basic captions. It's not going to frame you properly, add B-roll, or make it feel cinematic.

So use the free tool to find what works — then put real effort into the clips that are already winning.

Would you trust YouTube to pick your best moments? Let me know.

## TOPIC 2 — Phota: The AI App That "Re-Shoots" Your Phone Photos

### What Happened
On Nov 12, 2026, Phota Labs launched Phota, a free iPhone app that acts as an AI "photo assistant." It looks through your camera roll and suggests better versions of your photos — for example turning a wide group shot into a tighter portrait, or making a selfie look like someone else took it — and you can pick styles from subtle to dramatic (different angles, fish-eye looks). The company says it keeps people's faces recognizable and adds an invisible watermark marking edits as AI-altered. The founders previously worked at Adobe on computational photography, and the company raised a $5.6M seed round led by a16z.

### Why It Matters
This goes past "editing" into generating a version of a moment that the camera never actually captured — angle, framing and lens look included. It pushes the "is this still a real photo?" debate into everyday family and social photos, and it raises the question of what people will still pay a photographer for.

### Why It Suits Me
I'm a working photographer (Pixel With Bliss), so people genuinely want to hear how photographers feel about apps like this. I can bring the real-shoot perspective: what an actual photographer does at an event that an app can't fake.

### Hook
"There's a new free app that takes your bad phone photo and basically re-shoots it with AI — as a photographer, here's how I feel about that."

### Talking Points
- Phota (free, iPhone, launched Nov 12) scans your camera roll and turns snapshots into "professional-looking" shots — new angles, tighter framing, different lens looks.
- It says it keeps your face accurate and adds an invisible watermark so edits are flagged as AI-altered.
- The big question: if it changes the angle and lens, is that still a photo of what happened — or a recreation of it?

### My Angle
I'm not going to pretend this is the end of photography — the founders even say their target is people with messy camera rolls, not pros. But I'll draw a line: improving a photo and inventing a photo are different things. For memories, a lot of people won't care. For events, weddings and businesses, clients pay for a real moment captured well — real light, real expressions. Give credit for the watermark, and ask the audience where their line is.

### 30–60 Second Script
There's a new free app that takes your bad phone photo and basically re-shoots it with AI. As a photographer, here's how I feel about that.

It's called Phota. It just launched on iPhone. It looks through your camera roll and suggests better versions of your pictures. A wide group shot becomes a tight portrait. A selfie looks like someone else took it. You can even change the angle or the lens look.

And honestly? The tech is impressive. They say it keeps your face looking like you, and it adds an invisible watermark so the photo is marked as AI-edited. I respect that part.

But here's my line. Fixing a photo is one thing. Changing the angle and the lens? That's a photo of a moment the camera never actually saw.

For your camera roll, maybe that's fine. But when people hire me for an event, they want what really happened — real light, real faces, real moments.

So where's your line? Would you use this on your family photos?

## TOPIC 3 — Brands Will Spend $44B on Creators This Year — But Deals Still Have No Rulebook

### What Happened
On Nov 10, 2026, the IAB (Interactive Advertising Bureau, the US ad-industry body) launched its Trusted Creator Brand Deal Initiative and released a report, "The Creator Economy's Next Chapter." It says US creator ad spend is expected to reach $44 billion this year, but the systems for measuring, contracting and paying creators haven't kept up. In its survey of 53 industry leaders, 67% named measurement as the biggest problem, 66% want a standard definition of a "view", 61% pointed to usage rights as the top contract friction, and 60% said long payment terms cause real harm.

### Why It Matters
The money is real and growing, but small creators get squeezed by unclear usage rights, slow payments and "what counts as a view?" arguments. IAB plans to work on standard metrics (with the Media Rating Council) and a creator advisory body in 2027 — which could eventually shape how brand deals are written. Note: the survey is small and leans toward agencies/intermediaries, so IAB itself calls the numbers directional.

### Why It Suits Me
I'm trying to land my first paying clients with Pixel With Bliss. Usage rights and payment terms are exactly the things beginner creators and photographers get burned on, so this is a practical "protect yourself" video that comes from where I actually am.

### Hook
"Brands are about to spend 44 billion dollars on creators this year — and most of us still don't know how to write a deal."

### Talking Points
- The IAB says US brands will spend about $44B on creators this year, growing faster than the rest of media.
- Even the big players admit the basics are broken: measurement, what a "view" means, usage rights, and slow payments.
- If you're small: put usage rights, deliverables and payment dates in writing before you start.

### My Angle
Turn industry news into a beginner's checklist. I'm early in this too — so instead of "look at this big number," the point is: if even the pros say contracts and payments are messy, small creators need to protect themselves. Three things I'm doing for my own clients: define where and how long they can use the content, set a payment date (and a deposit), and agree what results you'll report.

### 30–60 Second Script
Brands are about to spend forty-four billion dollars on creators this year — and most of us still don't know how to write a deal.

That number comes from the IAB, the big US advertising industry group. They just put out a report, and the interesting part isn't the money. It's what they admit is broken.

People in the industry say measuring results is the number one problem. Most of them want a standard definition of what a "view" even is. Usage rights are the biggest fight in contracts. And a lot of them agree that slow payments hurt creators.

So if the big players are struggling with this, imagine being a small creator.

Here's what I'm doing as I land my first clients. One — write down exactly where and for how long they can use my content. Two — set a payment date, and take a deposit. Three — agree up front what results I'll report back.

The money's there. Just don't do the deal on a handshake.
